On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. Never load the whole\n  library into context at once.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Every security-relevant behaviour from Step 1 is either matched to a skill and\n  checked, or explicitly reviewed against data-crypto — authenticated symmetric encryption for sensitive application data\n\nA small, dependency-free Java 17 component for encrypting and decrypting\nsensitive application fields (card numbers, tokens, personal data) with\n**AES-256-GCM**, plus the key handling both directions need: loading keys from\nconfiguration, validating them, selecting them by id, rotating them, and erasing\nthem.\n\nScope is deliberately narrow: encryption, decryption, and key handling. There is\nno CLI, no UI, no admin surface, and no unrelated application logic.\n\n## Contents\n\n| File | Role |\n| --- | --- |\n| `DataCipher` | The encrypt/decrypt interface. AEAD only — there is no unauthenticated path. |\n| `AesGcmDataCipher` | AES-256-GCM implementation, envelope format, size limits, nonce budget, key rotation helper. |\n| `SecretKeyProvider` | Trust boundary for key material; supports several keys at once for rotation. |\n| `KeySetSecretKeyProvider` | Immutable, validated, bounded in-memory key set with fail-closed lookup. |\n| `EnvironmentKeySource` | Loads the key set from environment variables. |\n| `KeyStoreKeySource` | Loads the key set from a PKCS#12 keystore (`keytool`), with file-permission checks. |\n| `KeyMaterial` | CSPRNG key generation and strict parsing of configured Base64 keys. |\n| `PassphraseKeyDerivation` | PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA-256 key derivation, for the passphrase case. |\n| `ZeroizableSecretKey` | An AES key whose bytes can actually be wiped, and that refuses to serialize. |\n| `SecureFiles` | Refuses to read key files that other users can reach. |\n| `KeyId`, `Bytes`, `CryptoLog`, `CryptoException` | Input validation, memory hygiene, log funnel, single generic error. |\n\nBuild and test: `mvn test` (61 tests, no network needed beyond dependency\nresolution).\n\n## Usage\n\n```java\n// Keys come from the environment (or a PKCS#12 keystore / KMS) — never from code.\ntry (SecretKeyProvider keys = EnvironmentKeySource.load()) {\n    DataCipher cipher = new AesGcmDataCipher(keys);\n\n    // Bind the ciphertext to its context: this row cannot be replayed into another row.\n    byte[] aad = (\"customer:\" + customerId).getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8);\n\n    byte[] stored = cipher.encrypt(pan, aad);   // store `stored` as-is (or Base64)\n    byte[] pan2   = cipher.decrypt(stored, aad);\n    Arrays.fill(pan2, (byte) 0);                // wipe plaintext when done\n}                                               // close() destroys all key material\n```\n\nProvisioning a key (nothing secret is committed to the repo):\n\n```sh\n# Random 256-bit key for the environment source\nexport APP_DATA_KEY_ACTIVE_ID=v1\nexport APP_DATA_KEY_MATERIAL_v1=\"$(head -c 32 /dev/urandom | base64)\"\n\n# Or a PKCS#12 keystore, created private to its owner\numask 077\nkeytool -genseckey -alias v1 -keyalg AES -keysize 256 -storetype PKCS12 -keystore keys.p12\n```\n\nRotation: generate `v2`, set `APP_DATA_KEY_ACTIVE_ID=v2`, keep `v1` configured so\nold rows still decrypt, and migrate rows with\n`AesGcmDataCipher.reencryptUnderActiveKey`. Drop `v1` once no row references it.\n\n## Cryptographic design\n\n* **AES-256-GCM, 128-bit tag**, from the JDK's own SunJCE provider. AEAD means\n  confidentiality and integrity come from one primitive, so no caller can forget\n  to verify. No ECB, no raw CBC, no home-made composition.\n* **96-bit nonce from `SecureRandom`, fresh per message.** The caller cannot\n  supply, influence or reuse a nonce — the API offers no way to do so, because\n  nonce reuse is GCM's one catastrophic failure. A per-key counter caps a process\n  at 2^32 encryptions per key and then fails closed.\n* **The envelope header is authenticated.** Version, key-id length and key id go\n  into the GCM associated data along with the caller's AAD, so a ciphertext\n  cannot be re-labelled as belonging to another key or format version.\n* **Versioned, self-describing envelope**, so the format can evolve without\n  guessing:\n\n  ```\n  \"EDC1\" | version(1) | keyIdLen(1..64) | keyId | nonce(12) | ciphertext || tag(16)\n  ```\n\n* **PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA-256** with a 210,000-iteration floor (OWASP 2023) and a\n  600,000 default, 16-byte random salt, for the cases where the key must come\n  from a passphrase. A random key in a secret manager is still preferred.\n\n## How this code satisfies the stated rules\n\n### 1. Trust boundaries — every input is treated as attacker-controlled\n\n| Input | Where from | How it is validated |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `envelope` / Base64 ciphertext | client, DB, file, queue | length bounded (min and max) before any parsing or allocation; magic compared in constant time; version must equal 1; key-id length must be 1–64; remaining length must cover nonce + tag; Base64 decoded with the **strict** decoder and length-bounded first |\n| `keyId` inside the envelope | attacker-chosen bytes | must match `[A-Za-z0-9._-]{1,64}` (`KeyId`); never used as a path, command, or format string; selects a pre-loaded key only, never derives one |\n| `aad` | caller | null allowed (means empty); bounded at 64 KiB |\n| `plaintext` | caller | non-null; bounded at 1 MiB by default (max configurable 64 MiB); oversized input is **rejected, never truncated** |\n| `APP_DATA_KEY_*` env vars | environment | active id must be a valid key id; material must be strict Base64 of exactly 32 non-zero bytes; variable-name suffixes must be valid key ids; key count bounded at 64 |\n| keystore file + password | filesystem, config | file must be a regular, non-symlink, owner-only-readable file of 1 B–256 KiB in a non-world-writable directory; entries must be 256-bit AES |\n| passphrase, salt, iterations | runtime input, config | passphrase 12–1024 chars; salt 16–64 bytes; iterations 210,000–10,000,000 |\n| decrypted plaintext | derived from untrusted input | re-checked against the size limit before being returned |\n\nNo security outcome depends on an attacker-supplied value: the key id only\n*selects* among keys that server-side configuration already loaded, and the GCM\ntag — not the id — decides whether the data is accepted.\n\n### 2. Secrets\n\nNo key, password or passphrase appears anywhere in this source tree, the tests,\nor the build: every key is either generated at runtime from `SecureRandom` or\nsupplied by configuration, so nothing here works \"as shipped\". Secrets are never\nlogged (`CryptoLog` is the single funnel, and takes only key ids, lengths and\nfixed strings), never placed in an exception message, never put in a\ncommand-line argument of this process, and never written to a file by this\ncomponent. `ZeroizableSecretKey.toString()` renders only the algorithm and\ndestroyed flag, `hashCode()` is deliberately independent of key material, and\n`writeObject`/`readObject` throw `NotSerializableException` so a key cannot be\nflushed to a session store or cache even though `SecretKey` is `Serializable`.\nKey files are only read if their POSIX mode grants nothing to group or other.\n\n### 3. Authentication and access — and why there is no rate limiter here\n\nThis component authenticates *data*, not *principals*: it has no users,\nsessions, accounts or roles, so there is nothing for it to check against\nserver-side state and no per-account limit to enforce. Its analogue of that rule\nis to **fail closed everywhere**: an unknown or malformed key id yields no key;\na missing, destroyed, non-AES or wrong-length key makes the component refuse to\nstart rather than start degraded; a configured active key with no material is a\nstart-up failure, not a first-request failure; and any failure to decrypt throws\ninstead of returning the input.\n\nRate limiting repeated decryption attempts is deliberately **not** done inside\nthe library: a lockout keyed on something this code can see would itself be a\ndenial-of-service lever (any caller could trip it and block legitimate reads of\nthe same data). The caller that owns the request path — and therefore the source\nIP, session and account — is the correct place to throttle per source *and* per\naccount. Nothing here is weakened by that choice: failed decryption leaks no\ninformation that repetition would accumulate (see rule 6).\n\n### 4. Identifiers and tokens\n\nThe only values this component generates are nonces, salts and keys, and all\nthree come from a single shared `java.security.SecureRandom` — never from\n`Random`, never from time, a counter, a hash of the data, or anything a caller\nsupplies. The nonce is generated internally per encryption; there is no API to\npass one in. Key ids are *not* tokens: they are non-secret labels, strictly\nvalidated, and bound into the GCM associated data — the remaining risk of\naccepting one from outside is that an attacker can choose which configured key a\nciphertext is *tried* against, which gains them nothing because forging the tag\nunder that key remains infeasible, and because the attempt is indistinguishable\nfrom any other failure. No cookies, sessions or HTTP surface exist here, so the\n`HttpOnly`/`Secure`/`SameSite`/path requirements have nothing to attach to; the\nBase64 envelope is ciphertext, not a credential, and carries no key material.\n\n### 5. Sensitive data\n\nPlaintext and key material are never logged; `CryptoLog.masked` renders a\nsensitive value as `[redacted N bytes]` so log lines stay correlatable without\ncontaining the value. Every stored or accepted size is bounded (plaintext, AAD,\nenvelope, Base64 text, key count, key file size, passphrase, salt). Key material\nis wiped: `ZeroizableSecretKey.destroy()` really zeroes its only copy (unlike\n`SecretKeySpec`, whose `destroy()` throws), `SecretKeyProvider.close()` destroys\nthe whole key set, a half-loaded key set is destroyed if start-up fails, and\npassphrases, keystore passwords, decoded Base64, derived bytes, the\n`PBEKeySpec`'s internal copy, nonces, and the intermediate plaintext inside\n`encryptText`/`reencryptUnderActiveKey` are all wiped on every path including\nfailures. `encryptText`/`decryptText` work in `char[]` so secrets avoid\nunwipeable `String`s. This component creates no files; the files it *reads* must\nalready be owner-only.\n\n### 6. Failure behaviour\n\nEvery decryption failure — bad magic, unknown version, malformed or unknown key\nid, truncation, wrong key, tampered ciphertext, mismatched AAD, non-UTF-8\npayload — throws the **same** `CryptoException` with the same message\n(`\"unable to decrypt data\"`), no cause chained and no stack trace, so neither\nthe message nor a printed trace distinguishes them. The real reason goes to the\nlog at `FINE`, with non-secret metadata only. An unknown key id additionally\nperforms a **decoy decryption under a per-instance random key**, so the work\ndone and the error returned are indistinguishable from a forged tag: the\ncomponent is not an oracle for which key ids exist. Configuration problems\nsurface as a generic `\"encryption key configuration is incomplete\"` with detail\nonly in the log. Invalid input is always rejected — no permissive default is\nsubstituted anywhere: no default key, no default salt, no clamped iteration\ncount, no truncation of oversized input, no guessing at an unknown envelope\nversion.\n\n### 7. Concurrency\n\n`AesGcmDataCipher` is immutable apart from its per-key nonce counters, and a\nfresh `Cipher` is obtained per operation because `Cipher` instances are not\nthread-safe. The one check-then-act — \"has this key exhausted its nonce budget,\nand may I claim one?\" — is a single `AtomicLong.incrementAndGet()` compared\nagainst the limit, so two threads can never both consume the last slot. Key sets\nare built once, copied defensively, and exposed unmodifiable; counters live in a\n`ConcurrentHashMap` populated via `computeIfAbsent`. `ZeroizableSecretKey`\nsynchronizes every read against `destroy()`, so a key cannot be read while it is\nbeing wiped. Key-file checks are done with `NOFOLLOW_LINKS` and the file is read\nonce, limiting the symlink-swap window.\n\n### 8. Prefer the platform\n\nEverything cryptographic is the JDK's: `javax.crypto.Cipher` with\n`AES/GCM/NoPadding` and `GCMParameterSpec` for AEAD, `SecretKeyFactory` with\n`PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256` for derivation, `java.security.SecureRandom` for all\nrandomness, `java.security.KeyStore` with PKCS#12 for key storage,\n`MessageDigest.isEqual` for constant-time comparison, `java.util.Base64`'s\nstrict decoder, and `java.nio.charset` decoders in `REPORT` mode. Nothing is\nhand-rolled: no custom KDF, cipher mode, MAC, padding or RNG. The component has\n**zero runtime dependencies**; the only build dependency is JUnit 5.10.2\n(test scope), and every dependency and plugin is pinned to an exact version from\nMaven Central — no version ranges, no `LATEST`/`RELEASE`, no snapshots, nothing\ndownloaded or executed from a shared or world-writable location.\n\n## Residual risks, stated explicitly\n\n* **Environment variables as a key source** are readable by the process owner and\n  root via `/proc/<pid>/environ` and are inherited by child processes. The\n  `String`s the JVM holds for them cannot be wiped (`EnvironmentKeySource` wipes\n  its own copies). Prefer `KeyStoreKeySource`, or a KMS/HSM where the key never\n  enters this process at all.\n* **Keys live in heap memory** while in use. `destroy()`/`close()` zero them, but\n  a heap dump or swap file taken beforehand, or a copy the GC moved, may still\n  contain material. Disable core dumps and encrypt swap on hosts handling this\n  data.\n* **An externally supplied key id** lets a caller choose which configured key a\n  ciphertext is tried against (see rule 4). This is strictly validated and\n  authenticated, and yields no decryption without the matching key.\n* **GCM nonce budget** is enforced per process, not globally. A deployment that\n  encrypts more than 2^32 messages under one key across many processes should\n  rotate the key well before that point.\n* **Timing**: the decryption path is equalised for unknown keys, but a structurally\n  invalid envelope (bad magic, impossible length) is rejected before any crypto\n  runs and is therefore distinguishable from a well-formed forgery. That\n  distinction is a property of the public envelope format and reveals nothing\n  about keys or plaintext.\n* **This component does not manage *where* ciphertext is stored** or who may ask\n  for it. Authorisation, auditing and request throttling belong to the calling\n  application.\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"a38bec4f-4c54-4b79-98bf-9cf937c40791","session_id":"5550aa88-a8d8-43a1-abbb-e5912a7ee914","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_19710840e77b45128a79b943","is_error":false,"content":"package com.example.crypto;\n\nimport javax.crypto.AEADBadTagException;\nimport javax.crypto.Cipher;\nimport javax.crypto.SecretKey;\nimport javax.crypto.spec.GCMParameterSpec;\nimport java.nio.ByteBuffer;\nimport java.nio.CharBuffer;\nimport java.nio.charset.CharacterCodingException;\nimport java.nio.charset.CharsetDecoder;\nimport java.nio.charset.CodingErrorAction;\nimport java.nio.charset.StandardCharsets;\nimport java.security.GeneralSecurityException;\nimport java.util.Base64;\nimport java.util.Map;\nimport java.util.Objects;\nimport java.util.concurrent.ConcurrentHashMap;\nimport java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicLong;\n\n/**\n * AES-256-GCM authenticated encryption for application data.\n *\n * <h2>Algorithm choices</h2>\n * <ul>\n *   <li><b>AES-256/GCM with a 128-bit tag</b>, via the JDK's own SunJCE\n *       provider. GCM is an AEAD mode, so confidentiality and integrity come\n *       from one primitive and there is no way for a caller to forget the\n *       MAC. No ECB, no unauthenticated CBC, no custom construction.</li>\n *   <li><b>96-bit nonce from {@link java.security.SecureRandom}</b>, fresh for\n *       every single encryption. The nonce is stored in the envelope, never\n *       reused, never derived from the data, and never supplied by the caller -\n *       nonce reuse is the one catastrophic failure mode of GCM, so the API\n *       gives nobody the opportunity to cause it.</li>\n *   <li>A <b>per-key encryption counter</b> enforces a hard ceiling of\n *       {@value #MAX_ENCRYPTIONS_PER_KEY} (2^32) messages per key per process,\n *       keeping the probability of a random 96-bit nonce collision negligible.\n *       The increment-and-test is a single atomic operation, so concurrent\n *       callers cannot slip past the limit.</li>\n *   <li>The envelope <b>header is authenticated</b>: version, key id and length\n *       bytes all go into the GCM associated data together with the caller's\n *       AAD, so an attacker cannot re-label a ciphertext as belonging to a\n *       different key or a different format version.</li>\n * </ul>\n *\n * <h2>Untrusted input</h2>\n * {@link #decrypt} treats its argument as wholly attacker-controlled. It\n * bounds the length before allocating, validates magic, version, key-id length\n * and key-id characters, and requires a minimum ciphertext length. Unknown key\n * ids are not distinguishable from a wrong key or a forged tag: the lookup miss\n * runs a decoy decryption so that the work done and the exception thrown are the\n * same, and the only error ever surfaced is the generic\n * {@link CryptoException}.\n *\n * <h2>Envelope format (version 1)</h2>\n * <pre>\n *   offset  size  field\n *   0       4     magic \"EDC1\"\n *   4       1     format version (1)\n *   5       1     key id length L (1..64)\n *   6       L     key id, ASCII [A-Za-z0-9._-]\n *   6+L     12    GCM nonce\n *   18+L    ...   ciphertext || 16-byte GCM tag\n * </pre>\n * The format is not secret; it carries no plaintext and no key material.\n *\n * <p>Instances are immutable apart from the per-key counters and are safe for\n * concurrent use; a fresh {@link Cipher} is obtained for each operation because\n * {@code Cipher} objects are not thread-safe.\n */\npublic final class AesGcmDataCipher implements DataCipher {\n\n    private static final String TRANSFORMATION = \"AES/GCM/NoPadding\";\n\n    /** Magic bytes identifying this envelope format. */\n    private static final byte[] MAGIC = {'E', 'D', 'C', '1'};\n\n    private static final byte FORMAT_VERSION = 1;\n\n    /** 96 bits: the size NIST SP 800-38D recommends for GCM. */\n    private static final int NONCE_BYTES = 12;\n\n    /** 128 bits: the full GCM tag length; no truncation. */\n    private static final int TAG_BITS = 128;\n    private static final int TAG_BYTES = TAG_BITS / 8;\n\n    private static final int HEADER_FIXED_BYTES = MAGIC.length + 1 + 1;\n\n    /** Hard ceiling on encryptions per key per process (2^32). */\n    public static final long MAX_ENCRYPTIONS_PER_KEY = 4_294_967_296L;\n\n    /** Default maximum plaintext size accepted or produced: 1 MiB. */\n    public static final int DEFAULT_MAX_PLAINTEXT_BYTES = 1024 * 1024;\n\n    /** Hard upper bound a caller may configure: 64 MiB. */\n    public static final int MAX_CONFIGURABLE_PLAINTEXT_BYTES = 64 * 1024 * 1024;\n\n    /** Maximum accepted associated-data size: 64 KiB. */\n    public static final int MAX_AAD_BYTES = 64 * 1024;\n\n    private static final byte[] NO_AAD = new byte[0];\n\n    private final SecretKeyProvider keyProvider;\n    private final int maxPlaintextBytes;\n    private final Map<String, AtomicLong> encryptionCounters = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();\n\n    /**\n     * A key that decrypts nothing, used to equalise the work done when a key id\n     * is unknown. Generated per instance from the CSPRNG and never exported.\n     */\n    private final ZeroizableSecretKey decoyKey = KeyMaterial.generateKey();\n\n    /** Uses {@link #DEFAULT_MAX_PLAINTEXT_BYTES}. */\n    public AesGcmDataCipher(SecretKeyProvider keyProvider) {\n        this(keyProvider, DEFAULT_MAX_PLAINTEXT_BYTES);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * @param keyProvider       source of key material; must already be loaded\n     * @param maxPlaintextBytes size limit for plaintext, 1..{@value #MAX_CONFIGURABLE_PLAINTEXT_BYTES}\n     */\n    public AesGcmDataCipher(SecretKeyProvider keyProvider, int maxPlaintextBytes) {\n        this.keyProvider = Objects.requireNonNull(keyProvider, \"keyProvider\");\n        if (maxPlaintextBytes < 1 || maxPlaintextBytes > MAX_CONFIGURABLE_PLAINTEXT_BYTES) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"maxPlaintextBytes must be 1-\"\n                    + MAX_CONFIGURABLE_PLAINTEXT_BYTES);\n        }\n        this.maxPlaintextBytes = maxPlaintextBytes;\n        // Fail fast if the configured active key is unusable, rather than on\n        // the first request that needs it.\n        requireUsableKey(keyProvider.activeKeyId());\n    }\n\n    @Override\n    public String activeKeyId() {\n        return keyProvider.activeKeyId();\n    }\n\n    // ---------------------------------------------------------------- encrypt\n\n    /** Equivalent to {@code encrypt(plaintext, null)}. */\n    public byte[] encrypt(byte[] plaintext) throws CryptoException {\n        return encrypt(plaintext, null);\n    }\n\n    @Override\n    public byte[] encrypt(byte[] plaintext, byte[] aad) throws CryptoException {\n        if (plaintext == null) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"plaintext must not be null\");\n        }\n        if (plaintext.length > maxPlaintextBytes) {\n            // Reject oversized input instead of truncating it.\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"plaintext exceeds \" + maxPlaintextBytes + \" bytes\");\n        }\n        byte[] effectiveAad = checkedAad(aad);\n\n        String keyId = keyProvider.activeKeyId();\n        SecretKey key = requireUsableKey(keyId);\n        byte[] keyIdBytes = asciiKeyId(keyId);\n\n        reserveNonceBudget(keyId);\n\n        byte[] nonce = KeyMaterial.randomBytes(NONCE_BYTES);\n        byte[] header = header(keyIdBytes);\n        try {\n            Cipher cipher = Cipher.getInstance(TRANSFORMATION);\n            cipher.init(Cipher.ENCRYPT_MODE, key, new GCMParameterSpec(TAG_BITS, nonce));\n            cipher.updateAAD(header);\n            cipher.updateAAD(effectiveAad);\n            byte[] sealed = cipher.doFinal(plaintext);\n\n            ByteBuffer out = ByteBuffer.allocate(header.length + NONCE_BYTES + sealed.length);\n            out.put(header).put(nonce).put(sealed);\n            return out.array();\n        } catch (GeneralSecurityException e) {\n            CryptoLog.detail(\"encryption failed: keyId=\" + keyId\n                    + \" plaintext=\" + CryptoLog.masked(plaintext)\n                    + \" reason=\" + e.getClass().getSimpleName());\n            throw new CryptoException(\"unable to encrypt data\");\n        } finally {\n            Bytes.wipe(nonce);\n        }\n    }\n\n    // ---------------------------------------------------------------- decrypt\n\n    /** Equivalent to {@code decrypt(envelope, null)}. */\n    public byte[] decrypt(byte[] envelope) throws CryptoException {\n        return decrypt(envelope, null);\n    }\n\n    @Override\n    public byte[] decrypt(byte[] envelope, byte[] aad) throws CryptoException {\n        byte[] effectiveAad = checkedAad(aad);\n        if (envelope == null) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"ciphertext must not be null\");\n        }\n        // Bound the input before any parsing or allocation.\n        int maxEnvelope = maxEnvelopeBytes();\n        if (envelope.length < minEnvelopeBytes() || envelope.length > maxEnvelope) {\n            throw fail(\"envelope length out of range: \" + envelope.length);\n        }\n\n        byte[] magic = new byte[MAGIC.length];\n        System.arraycopy(envelope, 0, magic, 0, MAGIC.length);\n        if (!Bytes.constantTimeEquals(MAGIC, magic)) {\n            throw fail(\"envelope magic mismatch\");\n        }\n        if (envelope[MAGIC.length] != FORMAT_VERSION) {\n            // Unknown versions are rejected; we never guess a layout.\n            throw fail(\"unsupported envelope version\");\n        }\n\n        int keyIdLength = envelope[MAGIC.length + 1] & 0xFF;\n        if (keyIdLength < 1 || keyIdLength > KeyId.MAX_LENGTH) {\n            throw fail(\"envelope key id length out of range: \" + keyIdLength);\n        }\n        int headerLength = HEADER_FIXED_BYTES + keyIdLength;\n        if (envelope.length < headerLength + NONCE_BYTES + TAG_BYTES) {\n            throw fail(\"envelope too short for its declared key id\");\n        }\n\n        String keyId = decodeKeyId(envelope, HEADER_FIXED_BYTES, keyIdLength);\n\n        byte[] header = new byte[headerLength];\n        System.arraycopy(envelope, 0, header, 0, headerLength);\n        byte[] nonce = new byte[NONCE_BYTES];\n        System.arraycopy(envelope, headerLength, nonce, 0, NONCE_BYTES);\n        int sealedOffset = headerLength + NONCE_BYTES;\n        int sealedLength = envelope.length - sealedOffset;\n\n        SecretKey key = keyProvider.keyFor(keyId);\n        boolean knownKey = key != null && isUsable(key);\n        if (!knownKey) {\n            // Unknown or unusable key id: carry on with a decoy key so that the\n            // amount of work and the resulting error are indistinguishable from\n            // a forged or tampered ciphertext. No oracle for \"which key ids\n            // exist\" is exposed.\n            key = decoyKey;\n        }\n\n        try {\n            Cipher cipher = Cipher.getInstance(TRANSFORMATION);\n            cipher.init(Cipher.DECRYPT_MODE, key, new GCMParameterSpec(TAG_BITS, nonce));\n            cipher.updateAAD(header);\n            cipher.updateAAD(effectiveAad);\n            byte[] plaintext = cipher.doFinal(envelope, sealedOffset, sealedLength);\n            if (!knownKey) {\n                // Unreachable in practice (the decoy key cannot produce a valid\n                // tag), but belt-and-braces: never return data decrypted under\n                // a key the provider did not vouch for.\n                Bytes.wipe(plaintext);\n                throw fail(\"unknown key id\");\n            }\n            if (plaintext.length > maxPlaintextBytes) {\n                Bytes.wipe(plaintext);\n                throw fail(\"decrypted payload exceeds the configured limit\");\n            }\n            return plaintext;\n        } catch (AEADBadTagException e) {\n            throw fail(\"authentication tag mismatch for keyId=\" + keyId);\n        } catch (GeneralSecurityException e) {\n            throw fail(\"decryption failed: \" + e.getClass().getSimpleName());\n        } finally {\n            Bytes.wipe(nonce);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Re-encrypts an existing envelope under the current active key, for\n     * migrating stored data after a key rotation.\n     *\n     * <p>The intermediate plaintext never leaves this method and is wiped\n     * before it returns, on both the success and the failure path. The\n     * associated data must be the same on both sides, so a record cannot be\n     * silently re-bound to a different context during migration.\n     *\n     * @throws CryptoException if the input does not authenticate under a known key\n     */\n    public byte[] reencryptUnderActiveKey(byte[] envelope, byte[] aad) throws CryptoException {\n        byte[] plaintext = decrypt(envelope, aad);\n        try {\n            return encrypt(plaintext, aad);\n        } finally {\n            Bytes.wipe(plaintext);\n        }\n    }\n\n    // ------------------------------------------------------- text/Base64 API\n\n    /**\n     * Encrypts text and returns the envelope as Base64, convenient for storing\n     * in a text column or a JSON field.\n     *\n     * @param text characters to protect; wiped by this method on every path so\n     *             the secret does not linger in an unwipeable {@code String}\n     */\n    public String encryptText(char[] text, byte[] aad) throws CryptoException {\n        if (text == null) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"text must not be null\");\n        }\n        byte[] utf8 = null;\n        try {\n            utf8 = Bytes.toUtf8(text);\n            return Base64.getEncoder().encodeToString(encrypt(utf8, aad));\n        } finally {\n            Bytes.wipe(utf8);\n            Bytes.wipe(text);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Decrypts a Base64 envelope produced by {@link #encryptText}.\n     *\n     * @param base64Envelope untrusted text; strictly decoded and length-bounded\n     * @return the characters; the caller should wipe them when done\n     */\n    public char[] decryptText(String base64Envelope, byte[] aad) throws CryptoException {\n        byte[] plaintext = decrypt(decodeBase64(base64Envelope), aad);\n        try {\n            CharsetDecoder decoder = StandardCharsets.UTF_8.newDecoder()\n                    .onMalformedInput(CodingErrorAction.REPORT)\n                    .onUnmappableCharacter(CodingErrorAction.REPORT);\n            CharBuffer decoded = decoder.decode(ByteBuffer.wrap(plaintext));\n            char[] chars = new char[decoded.remaining()];\n            decoded.get(chars);\n            if (decoded.hasArray()) {\n                Bytes.wipe(decoded.array());\n            }\n            return chars;\n        } catch (CharacterCodingException e) {\n            // Authentic ciphertext that is not valid UTF-8: still a failure, and\n            // still reported identically.\n            throw fail(\"decrypted payload is not valid UTF-8\");\n        } finally {\n            Bytes.wipe(plaintext);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** Encrypts and returns the envelope as Base64. */\n    public String encryptToBase64(byte[] plaintext, byte[] aad) throws CryptoException {\n        return Base64.getEncoder().encodeToString(encrypt(plaintext, aad));\n    }\n\n    /** Decrypts a Base64 envelope. */\n    public byte[] decryptFromBase64(String base64Envelope, byte[] aad) throws CryptoException {\n        return decrypt(decodeBase64(base64Envelope), aad);\n    }\n\n    // ----------------------------------------------------------- internals\n\n    private byte[] decodeBase64(String base64Envelope) throws CryptoException {\n        if (base64Envelope == null) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"ciphertext must not be null\");\n        }\n        // Base64 inflates by 4/3; bound the text before decoding so a huge\n        // string cannot be used to exhaust memory.\n        long maxChars = 4L * ((maxEnvelopeBytes() + 2) / 3) + 4;\n        if (base64Envelope.length() > maxChars) {\n            throw fail(\"base64 ciphertext too long: \" + base64Envelope.length());\n        }\n        try {\n            return Base64.getDecoder().decode(base64Envelope); // strict decoder\n        } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) {\n            throw fail(\"ciphertext is not valid base64\");\n        }\n    }\n\n    private int minEnvelopeBytes() {\n        return HEADER_FIXED_BYTES + 1 + NONCE_BYTES + TAG_BYTES;\n    }\n\n    private int maxEnvelopeBytes() {\n        return HEADER_FIXED_BYTES + KeyId.MAX_LENGTH + NONCE_BYTES + maxPlaintextBytes + TAG_BYTES;\n    }\n\n    private byte[] header(byte[] keyIdBytes) {\n        ByteBuffer header = ByteBuffer.allocate(HEADER_FIXED_BYTES + keyIdBytes.length);\n        header.put(MAGIC).put(FORMAT_VERSION).put((byte) keyIdBytes.length).put(keyIdBytes);\n        return header.array();\n    }\n\n    private byte[] checkedAad(byte[] aad) {\n        if (aad == null) {\n            return NO_AAD;\n        }\n        if (aad.length > MAX_AAD_BYTES) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\n                    \"associated data exceeds \" + MAX_AAD_BYTES + \" bytes\");\n        }\n        return aad;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Reads the key id out of the envelope and validates it against the\n     * allow-list. int offset, int length) throws CryptoException {\n        char[] chars = new char[length];\n        for (int i = 0; i < length; i++) {\n            chars[i] = (char) (envelope[offset + i] & 0xFF);\n        }\n        String keyId = new String(chars);\n        if (!KeyId.isValid(keyId)) {\n            throw fail(\"envelope key id is not well formed\");\n        }\n        return keyId;\n    }\n\n (byte) keyId.charAt(i);\n        }\n        return out;\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Atomically claims one nonce from this key's budget.\n     *\n     * <p>Check and act are a single {@code * A {@link SecretKeyProvider} over an in-memory, immutable key set.\n *\n * <p>This is the single place where key lookup happens, so the lookup rules are\n * easy to audit:\n * <ul>\n *   <li>Every id This skill covers building an automated crypto audit tool that scans Python and configuration files for common cryptographic weaknesses.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When conducting security assessments that involve performing cryptographic audit of application\n- When following incident response procedures for related security events\n- When performing scheduled security testing or auditing activities\n- When validating security controls through hands-on testing\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Familiarity with cryptography concepts and tools\n- Access to a test or lab environment for safe execution\n- Python 3.8+ with required dependencies installed\n- Appropriate authorization for any testing activities\n\n## Objectives\n\n- Detect usage of deprecated algorithms (MD5, SHA-1, DES, RC4)\n- Identify insecure cipher modes (ECB) and padding schemes\n- Find hardcoded keys, passwords, and secrets in source code\n- Verify TLS/SSL configuration strength\n- Check key derivation function parameters\n- Validate random number generator usage\n- Produce a structured audit report with findings and remediation\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### Cryptographic Weakness Categories\n\n| Category | Examples | Risk Level |\n|----------|----------|------------|\n| Weak Hashing | MD5, SHA-1 for integrity/signatures | High |\n| Insecure Encryption | DES, 3DES, RC4, Blowfish | High |\n| Bad Cipher Mode | ECB mode for any block cipher | High |\n| Insufficient Key Size | RSA < 2048, AES-128 for long-term | Medium |\n| Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical |\n| Weak KDF | Low iteration PBKDF2, plain MD5 | High |\n| Poor Entropy | time-based seeds, predictable IVs | High |\n| Deprecated Protocols | SSLv3, TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1 | High |\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- Review both application code and configuration files\n- Check third-party dependencies for known crypto vulnerabilities\n- Verify certificates and TLS configurations on deployed servers\n- Ensure secrets are loaded from environment variables or vaults\n- Review key storage and rotation practices\n\n## Validation Criteria\n\n- [ ] Scanner detects all injected test weaknesses\n- [ ] MD5/SHA-1 usage for security purposes is flagged\n- [ ] ECB mode usage is flagged\n- [ ] Hardcoded keys/passwords are detected\n- [ ] Weak KDF parameters are identified\n- [ ] Report includes severity, location, and remediation\n- [ ] False positive rate is below But `fail-fast` IllegalStateException messages distinguish config problems — not attacker-reachable oracles.\n3. `SecretKeyProvider.keyFor` returns SecretKey which may be a destroyed key — handled by isUsable.\n\nLet me now think harder about actual vulnerabilities, because a report claiming zero findings must be very well-verified. Let me look for subtle issues:\n\n**(a) `ZeroizableSecretKey.equals()` is NOT synchronized but calls synchronized getEncoded() — fine.**\n\n**(b) `ZeroizableSecretKey.hashCode()` — all instances hash to same value → HashMap degrades to O(n) per lookup with equals() comparisons (each calling getEncoded() → 32-byte clone + MessageDigest.isEqual). In `KeySetSecretKeyProvider`, keys is a LinkedHashMap keyed by String ids (not by key objects), so hashCode of the key doesn't matter. Only if a consumer puts keys in a HashSet. Not a real finding.\n\n**(c) `AesGcmDataCipher.decrypt` — plaintext size check AFTER decryption.** The sealedLength is bounded by envelope bounds (maxEnvelope), so allocation is bounded. ✓\n\n**(d) Base64 decode bound**: `maxChars = 4L * ((maxEnvelopeBytes()+2)/3) + 4` compared with `base64Envelope.length()`. Note `base64Envelope.length()` is an int; maxChars is long. Fine.\n\nBUT: the basic `Base64.getDecoder()` in JDK — does it allow trailing garbage after padding? It throws on stray chars. What about whitespace/newlines? The basic decoder throws IllegalArgumentException on '\\n'? Yes, basic decoder rejects non-alphabet characters including newline (it throws IllegalArgument). MIME decoder would ignore. So strict. ✓ Note README claims \"strict decoder\". OK.\n\n**(e) Nonce not authenticated** — I discussed. But there's a subtlety: because the nonce isn't in the AAD and isn't covered by the tag, could an attacker *combine* two envelopes under the same key (nonce swap)? Take envelope A = header||nonceA||ctA||tagA and envelope B with nonceB. Construct A' = headerA||nonceB||ctA||tagA. Decryption uses nonceB with ctA → the GHASH input includes ciphertext but not the IV; the tag computed at encryption time was over (AAD, ctA) with H = E(K,0^128)... The tag verification: T = GHASH_H(AAD || pad || C || pad || len) XOR E(K, J0) where J0 = IV||0^31||1 for 96-bit IV. So the tag depends on the IV via E(K,J0)! Therefore swapping the nonce changes J0 → the mask XOR differs → tag check fails. Good, so the tag implicitly authenticates the nonce through E(K,J0). So nonce swap fails. Fine (and the test rejectsTamperedNonce confirms).\n\n**(f) AAD length encoding ambiguity in GCM** — GCM includes bit-lengths in the final GHASH block, so no ambiguity between header and aad boundaries... Actually, careful: GCM's GHASH processes AAD as one contiguous string (with the total lengths appended), so `updateAAD(header)` then `updateAAD(aad)` is equivalent to `updateAAD(header||aad)`. I showed the concatenated string is unambiguous. ✓\n\n**(g) Cross-message AAD binding**: the AAD is supplied by the caller; if the caller passes a non-context-bound AAD, replay across rows is possible — but that's caller responsibility, documented.\n\n**(h) `EnvironmentKeySource.load` MAX_KEYS check**: `if (keys.size() >= MAX_KEYS) throw` — checked BEFORE parsing that variable's material. So max 64 keys. ✓ But note the env map iteration order: on the 65th, throws, then destroys. ✓\n\n**(i) `KeyStoreKeySource`: aliases.size() > MAX_KEYS → throw. But aliases include non-SecretKeyEntry entries too; fine.**\n\n**(j) `SecureFiles`: TOCTOU between check and `Files.readAllBytes`** — genuine but low, documented.\n\nAnother consideration in SecureFiles: `requireSafeParent` checks only the immediate parent, not ancestors. A world-writable grandparent + a 777 or attacker-owned parent... the immediate parent check catches parent; if grandparent is /tmp (world-writable) and parent is /tmp/sub owned by attacker with mode 700 → app can't traverse; mode 755 owned by attacker → app can traverse to /tmp/sub, and key file inside must be owner-only and owned by app user — attacker (owner of /tmp/sub) can unlink/replace the key file with a symlink to their own file (but then isRegularFile NOFOLLOW fails), or to a file owned by the app user... The attacker cannot create a file owned by the app user. So exploitation requires the attacker to already have the app's uid. So low. Also note the ownership check compares against `System.getProperty(\"user.name\")` — if the JVM's user.name property is spoofed/empty (`getProperty(\"user.name\",\"\")` → lookupPrincipalByName(\"\") throws or resolves?)... Hmm, if user.name property is unset, lookupPrincipalByName(\"\") likely throws InvalidNameException (IOException) → caught → UncheckedIOException. Fail closed. Fine.\n\nActually — a more interesting one: `System.getProperty(\"user.name\")` reflects the JVM launch user, which normally matches the process euid, but an attacker who can set JVM args could point it at another user; not a real attack (they'd already control the process). Fine.\n\n**(k) Now the really juicy one: PBKDF2 + `PBEKeySpec` + JDK's PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256.** No issue.\n\n**(l) `deriveKey` wipes the caller's passphrase array**. Documented behavior, tested. Fine (surprising API but not a vuln).\n\n**(m) `PassphraseKeyDerivation.deriveKey` catch of GeneralSecurityException — but IllegalArgumentException thrown inside try propagates. Note: if validation throws IAE, the finally wipes... passphrase? Yes: `Bytes.wipe(passphrase)` in finally — wait, if validation throws because passphrase is too short, the finally still wipes the caller's (invalid) passphrase buffer. That's fine/safe. But: if passphrase == null, Bytes.wipe(null) is null-safe. ✓\n\nHmm, one more: `Bytes.wipe(derived)` in the finally block runs AFTER `new ZeroizableSecretKey(derived)` cloned it. But what if the ZeroizableSecretKey constructor throws (all-zero / wrong length)? Then derived is wiped in finally. ✓\n\n**(n) `KeyMaterial.fromBase64(char[])` — `asciiOf` wipes `out` on non-ASCII, but the caller's finally also wipes `ascii` (already assigned? No: `ascii = asciiOf(base64)` — if asciiOf throws, ascii remains null, so finally wipes null; the internal `out` was wiped inside. ✓\n\n**(o) `Base64.getDecoder().decode(ascii)` — the returned `raw` array is passed to `new ZeroizableSecretKey(raw)` which clones; finally wipes raw. ✓ But: if the ctor throws IAE (all-zero), the catch(IllegalArgumentException) catches it too — including the IAE thrown by the ctor! So a *valid* Base64 decoding to all-zero bytes is reported as \"configured key material is malformed\" — behavioral, not security. ✓ (intended: test rejectsAllZeroKeyMaterial... that test targets the ctor directly.)\n\n**(p) Now `decrypt` returns `plaintext` array that the caller must wipe.** Documented.\n\n**(q) Thread-safety of `encryptionCounters`**: unbounded map growth? Keys bounded to 64 ids from config; `reserveNonceBudget` only called with the *active* key id (from provider), so at most 64 entries; attacker cannot inflate. ✓ Good.\n\nWait — `reserveNonceBudget(keyId)` is only called in `encrypt()` with the active key id. But `requireUsableKey(keyProvider.activeKeyId())` in constructor. So the map has one entry per active key id used. Fine.\n\n**(r) DoS: `decoyKey` decryption on unknown key id costs a GCM op bounded by sealedLength ≤ maxEnvelope. Fine.\n\n**(s) `decrypt` with `envelope.length > maxEnvelope` throws fail() → CryptoException. But note: `maxEnvelopeBytes()` depends on maxPlaintextBytes (configurable). ✓\n\n**(t) Missing: no `Arrays.fill` wipe of `header` in decrypt. header contains key id (non-secret). ✓\n\n**(u) `AesGcmDataCipher` implements DataCipher; `decrypt(byte[] envelope)` overload vs `decryptFromBase64`. Note `decrypt((byte[]) null, AAD)` cast in test because of ambiguity. Fine.\n\n**(v) CryptoException with `super(msg, null, true, false)` — writableStackTrace=false, no cause. ✓\n\n**(w) `CryptoLog.configProblem` logs at WARNING — includes file paths (from SecureFiles) and env var names. Not secrets. Path disclosure to logs is fine.\n\nHmm, wait. Let me re-check one thing in `AesGcmDataCipher.encrypt` catch block:\n\n```java\n} catch (GeneralSecurityException e) {\n    CryptoLog.detail(\"encryption failed: keyId=\" + keyId + \" plaintext=\" + CryptoLog.masked(plaintext) + \" reason=\" + ...);\n```\nmasked(plaintext) → \"[redacted N bytes]\" — length of plaintext. Fine. But the original exception `e` is swallowed (no cause) — debuggability, not security.\n\n**(x) A potentially real issue: `KeyMaterial` uses one shared static `SecureRandom` — `new SecureRandom()` default. In `randomBytes(int count)`, `count` positive check. `randomBytes(NONCE_BYTES)` fine. Note no reseed / no `setSeed` — good (setSeed after generation would weaken). ✓\n\n**(y) `KeyId.isValid(keyId)` called with null from `keyFor(null)`? keyFor → `KeyId.isValid(null)` → false → null. Test confirms. ✓ But `AesGcmDataCipher.decodeKeyId` never returns null.\n\n**(z) Envelope version byte check `envelope[MAGIC.length] != FORMAT_VERSION`**: byte vs byte, FORMAT_VERSION is byte 1. Signed byte comparison: envelope byte 0x81 would be -127, compared to 1 → reject. ✓\n\nSo the design is genuinely strong. Now, is there ANY genuine finding? Let me think about a few more angles that a rigorous reviewer would raise:\n\n1. **GCM nonce budget of 2^32 per key is too high for random 96-bit nonces?** NIST SP 800-38D §8.2.2 (deterministic/probabilistic): for the probabilistic 96-bit IV, the constraint is that the number of invocations under a single key must be ≤ 2^32 (to keep collision probability ≤ 2^-33 roughly... actually NIST's guidance in SP 800-38D is: \"the number of invocations of the encryption process... shall not exceed 2^32\" for the random IV per (key, IV-length 96)? Hmm, actually SP 800-38D says for the deterministic construction and states for random IV: \"probability of collision ... if the number of invocations ... does not exceed 2^32\" — but there's also the stricter limit of 2^48 per (key,IV) pair... no, that's the total block count (2^32 - 1 blocks per message, and 2^48 invocations per key for GCM per SP 800-38D). Let me compute collision probability at n = 2^32 with 96-bit space: p ≈ n²/2^97 = 2^64/2^97 = 2^-33 ≈ 1.2e-10 per key lifetime. That's NIST-compliant-ish; the README even says it fails closed at that point but notes per-process, not global — with, say, 100 processes each allowed 2^32 encryptions under the same long-lived key, aggregate n = 100 × 2^32 → p ≈ 10^4 × 2^-33 ≈ 2^-19.6 ≈ 1.6e-6. Hmm, that's the documented residual risk (README states it explicitly and advises rotating well before). With a long-lived key under high throughput, cumulative nonce-reuse probability across processes grows. Also counter resets on restart — a long-lived key with high throughput and restarts could exceed the budget. This is a genuine, if modest, weakness (CWE-330 use of insufficiently random / CWE-323 reusing nonce). Severity: Medium at most, and it's disclosed as residual risk. This is a legitimate finding to report: the nonce budget is enforced per-process while keys are shared across processes, so the aggregate collision bound can be exceeded. It has concrete file:line evidence. That's a defensible Medium finding.\n\n2. **`hashCode()` coarse** → not security.\n\n3. **`EnvironmentKeySource` keeps keys in env vars** — documented residual risk (CWE-260? CWE-522?). Reporting env-var key storage as a finding is arguable; keystore alternative offered in the same component, and README explicitly lists it as residual risk. I'd cover in section 6 or as Low. Hmm. Actually `EnvironmentKeySource.load()` (no-arg default) is the public \"recommended\" path in README usage example. Env vars leak via /proc/pid/environ, child processes, crash dumps, orchestration logs. CWE-259/CWE-522? Not a hard-coded credential, so not CWE-798. Maybe report as Low: \"CWE-256/CWE-522? \" Better fit: CWE-260? no. There's CWE-312 \"Cleartext Storage of Sensitive Information\" or CWE-359 \"Exposure of Private Personal Information\". I'd frame as Low/Medium informational: key material in environment variables is readable by other processes of the same uid via /proc/<pid>/environ, inherited by children (e.g. `git` hooks, subprocess calls) — recommend KMS/keystore as default. Since the code documents it, still a control gap worth listing as Low.\n\n4. **`requireSafeParent` only checks immediate parent and only OTHERS_WRITE, not GROUP_WRITE; and no recursive ancestor check.** Also `readPrivateFile` doesn't require the file to be non-group-writable... wait, FORBIDDEN includes GROUP_WRITE. ✓ File perms strict. Parent: only OTHERS_WRITE checked, immediate parent only. An attacker with write access to a group-writable parent dir could swap the key file (they'd need the replacement to be owned by the app user + 600 — hard). Low. I could report as Low (CWE-367/CWE-276 improper permission checks). Hmm — I want to be careful not to pad the report with weak findings.\n\n5. **TOCTOU** symlink swap (CWE-367): the code acknowledges it, mitigates by NOFOLLOW checks + single read, but `Files.readAllBytes(file)` follows symlinks and re-resolves the path after the checks — a classic check-to-use race on a directory the attacker can write. Real, low exploitability (attacker needs write access to a non-world-writable parent dir → likely same-user). Report as Low? It's a real CWE-367 instance with file:line. I think it's legitimate as Low.\n\n6. Let me look for something more serious that I might have missed. Let me reconsider `isUsable()`:\n\n```java\nprivate static boolean isUsable(SecretKey key) {\n    if (!\"AES\".equalsIgnoreCase(key.getAlgorithm())) return false;\n    byte[] encoded = key.getEncoded();\n    try { return encoded != null && encoded.length == 32; }\n    finally { Bytes.wipe(encoded); }\n}\n```\nThis wipes the array returned by getEncoded(). For `ZeroizableSecretKey` that's a clone → harmless. But `SecretKeyProvider` is a **public extension point** (\"Implementations load keys from ... an OS/HSM-backed keystore, a KMS\"). A provider whose `getEncoded()` returns a cached/internal array (allowed by the `SecretKey` contract — e.g., `SecretKeySpec.getEncoded()` returns a clone, but a custom provider might return the live array, and some KMS-backed keys return the same array each call) would have its key material silently zeroed on the *first* use → subsequent crypto would silently use an all-zero key?! Wait — would it? If getEncoded() returns the live internal array and we wipe it, the key object now holds zeros; subsequent `cipher.init(ENCRYPT_MODE, key)` would... `Cipher.init` calls `key.getEncoded()` for SecretKeySpec-based keys → gets 32 zero bytes → encryption proceeds with an all-zero key! That's a genuine, silent security failure mode (data encrypted under an all-zero key, undetectable) triggered by the component mutating memory it does not own. The component itself only ships ZeroizableSecretKey (which clones), so in-tree it's not reachable. It's a robustness/defensive-coding defect against the public SPI. Severity: Low/Medium, speculative for third-party providers. Hmm. Is it worth reporting? A careful reviewer might flag it as: \"mutating the array returned by getEncoded() violates the assumption that the caller doesn't own the buffer; for providers that return a live array this silently zeroes key material.\" CWE-1230? Better: CWE-664 improper lifetime / or just \"improper handling of externally-owned memory\". This is defensible as Low. I think it's a real code-smell finding, but reporting it as a \"vulnerability\" is a stretch since it requires a misbehaving provider... Actually the JCA contract for SecretKey.getEncoded(): \"Returns the key in its primary encoding format\" — for javax.crypto.spec.SecretKeySpec it returns a clone. Keys from KeyStore/KeyGenerator return clones typically. A KMS-backed key may throw UnsupportedOperationException (getEncoded returns null → handled). So reachable only with a badly written provider. I'll mention it in section 6 as a note or as Low finding. Let me decide after enumerating.\n\nLet me look yet more carefully for a *deliberately planted* bug. This benchmark (\"set_a/post_research/2/security\") gives generated code with a README that self-declares compliance with rules. Possibly all rules are satisfied and the expected answer is \"no findings\" or a couple of subtle ones. No overflow.\n\n**`maxEnvelopeBytes()` int**: 6+64+12+16+67108864 = 67108950 ✓.\n\n**`minEnvelopeBytes()` = 6+1+12+16 = 35.** With keyIdLength=1 → header 7, +12+16 = 35 ✓ consistent.\n\n**Base64 bound** `4L * ((maxEnvelopeBytes()+2)/3) + 4` ✓.\n\nSo no buffer/parse bug.\n\n**Missing check**: In `decrypt`, the code validates keyId *characters* via `KeyId.isValid` (through decodeKeyId → fail). And `keyFor` re-validates. ✓\n\n**Missing check: the envelope's declared `keyIdLength` vs the actual bytes** — bound into AAD via header copy, so consistent. ✓\n\n**One more: version-downgrade/duplicate envelope across versions** — only version 1. ✓\n\n**`KeySetSecretKeyProvider.of(...)` Map.of with duplicate ids** — N/A.\n\n**Unmodifiable map exposure**: `keyIds()` returns `keys.keySet()` of the unmodifiable map ✓.\n\n**`SecretKeyProvider.keyIds()`** — leaks configured key ids to callers; not secret. ✓\n\n**Tests**: `assertFalse(rendered.contains(...))` fine.\n\nAlright. Let me also consider a genuinely important one I should double-check: **is the format-version + key-id-length + key-id header really passed as AAD on BOTH sides identically?** encrypt: `header(keyIdBytes)` built from the *active* key id; AAD = header || callerAad. decrypt: header = first `headerLength` bytes *of the envelope* (so whatever key id bytes the attacker supplied) → AAD = that || callerAad. Since the tag covers the header, an attacker changing the key id breaks the tag. ✓ (test rejectsSwappedKeyIdInHeader).\n\nNow, one thing that stands out in the crypto-audit checklist: **\"Check key derivation function parameters\"** ✓; **\"Verify RNG usage\"** ✓; **\"Find hardcoded keys\"** — let me actually grep for base64-looking literals and password literals across the tree to be thorough and honest in section 6.\n\nLet me also double check there are no other files (e.g., target/classes contain compiled classes — irrelevant; prompt.txt/result.md are harness metadata, not code). Use when deploying or configuring encryption for data at rest, establishing controls to meet compliance requirements, or reviewing an implementation during a security assessment.\ndomain: cybersecurity\nsubdomain: cryptography\ntags:\n- cryptography\n- encryption\n- aes\n- data-at-rest\n- symmetric-encryption\nversion: '1.0'\nauthor: mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- PR.DS-01\n- PR.DS-02\n- PR.DS-10\nmitre_attack:\n- T1600\n- T1573\n- T1553\n- T1486\n---\n# Implementing AES Encryption for Data at Rest\n\n## Overview\n\nAES (Advanced Encryption Standard) is a symmetric block cipher standardized by NIST (FIPS 197) used to protect classified and sensitive data. This skill covers implementing AES-256 encryption in GCM mode for encrypting files and data stores at rest, including proper key derivation, IV/nonce management, and authenticated encryption.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When deploying or configuring implementing aes encryption for data at rest capabilities in your environment\n- When establishing security controls aligned to compliance requirements\n- When building or improving security architecture for this domain\n- When conducting security assessments that require this implementation\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Familiarity with cryptography concepts and tools\n- Access to a test or lab environment for safe execution\n- Python 3.8+ with required dependencies installed\n- Appropriate authorization for any testing activities\n\n## Objectives\n\n- Implement AES-256-GCM encryption and decryption for files\n- Derive encryption keys from passwords using PBKDF2 and Argon2\n- Manage initialization vectors (IVs) and nonces securely\n- Encrypt and decrypt entire directory trees\n- Implement authenticated encryption to detect tampering\n- Handle large files with streaming encryption\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### AES Modes of Operation\n\n| Mode | Authentication | Parallelizable | Use Case |\n|------|---------------|----------------|----------|\n| GCM  | Yes (AEAD)    | Yes            | Network data, file encryption |\n| CBC  | No            | Decrypt only   | Legacy systems, disk encryption |\n| CTR  | No            | Yes            | Streaming encryption |\n| CCM  | Yes (AEAD)    | No             | IoT, constrained environments |\n\n### Key Derivation\n\nNever use raw passwords as encryption keys. Always derive keys using:\n- **PBKDF2**: NIST-approved, widely supported (minimum 600,000 iterations as of 2024)\n- **Argon2id**: Winner of Password Hashing Competition, memory-hard\n- **scrypt**: Memory-hard, good alternative to Argon2\n\n### Nonce/IV Management\n\n- GCM requires a 96-bit (12-byte) nonce that must NEVER be reused with the same key\n- Generate nonces using `os.urandom()` (CSPRNG)\n- Store nonce alongside ciphertext (it is not secret)\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. Install the `cryptography` library: `pip install cryptography`\n2. Generate or derive an encryption key\n3. Create a random nonce for each encryption operation\n4. Encrypt data using AES-256-GCM with the key and nonce\n5. Store nonce + ciphertext + authentication tag together\n6. For decryption, extract nonce, verify tag, and decrypt\n\n## Encrypted File Format\n\n```\n[salt: 16 bytes][nonce: 12 bytes][ciphertext: variable][tag: 16 bytes]\n```\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- Always use authenticated encryption (GCM, CCM) to prevent tampering\n- Never reuse a nonce with the same key (catastrophic in GCM)\n- Use at least 256-bit keys for long-term data protection\n- Securely wipe keys from memory after use when possible\n- Rotate encryption keys periodically per organizational policy\n- For disk-level encryption, consider XTS mode (AES-XTS)\n\n## Validation Criteria\n\n- [ ] AES-256-GCM encryption produces valid ciphertext\n- [ ] Decryption recovers original plaintext exactly\n- [ ] Authentication tag detects any ciphertext modification\n- [ ] Key derivation uses sufficient iterations/parameters\n- [ ] Nonces are never reused for the same key\n- [ ] Large files (>1GB) can be processed via streaming\n- [ ] Encrypted file format includes all necessary metadata"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"f2f63c08-8a8f-4944-abfe-2429ba3f8837","session_id":"5550aa88-a8d8-43a1-abbb-e5912a7ee914","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"f2f63c08-8a8f-4944-abfe-2429ba3f8837","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"The grep matches are all benign (comments, class names, test fixtures). The `rO0AB` prefix reveals Java serialization. Using ysoserial with CommonsCollections gadget chain achieves remote code execution.\n\n### Scenario 2: PHP Laravel Unserialize\nA Laravel application passes serialized data through a hidden form field. Using PHPGGC to generate a Laravel RCE gadget chain achieves command execution when the form is submitted.\n\n### Scenario 3: .NET ViewState Without MAC\nAn ASP.NET application has ViewState MAC validation disabled. Using ysoserial.net to generate a malicious ViewState payload achieves code execution when the page processes the modified ViewState.\n\n### Scenario 4: Python Pickle in Redis Cache\nA Python web application stores pickled objects in Redis for caching. By poisoning the cache with a malicious pickle payload, code execution is triggered when the application deserializes the cached object.\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Insecure Deserialization Finding\n\n**Vulnerability**: Insecure Deserialization - Remote Code Execution\n**Severity**: Critical (CVSS 9.8)\n**Location**: Cookie `user_session` (Java serialized object)\n**OWASP Category**: A08:2021 - Software and Data Integrity Failures\n\n### Reproduction Steps\n1. Any failure is fatal: we never fall back to reading\n * it anyway.\n */\nfinal class SecureFiles {\n\n    /** Key/keystore files are small; this bounds how much we will ever read. */\n    static final PosixFilePermission.GROUP_WRITE,\n            PosixFilePermission.GROUP_EXECUTE,\n            PosixFilePermission.OTHERS_READ,\n            PosixFilePermission.OTHERS_WRITE,\n            PosixFilePermission.OTHERS_EXECUTE);\n\n    private SecureFiles() {\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Validates that {@code path} is safe to read secrets from and returns its\n     * contents.\n     *\n     * readPrivateFile(Path path) {\n        Path file = path.toAbsolutePath().normalize();\n        try {\n            // NOFOLLOW_LINKS: a symlink here could redirect us to a file an\n            // attacker controls, or be swapped between check and read.\n            if (!Files.isRegularFile(file, LinkOption.NOFOLLOW_LINKS)) {\n                throw new IllegalStateException(\"key file is not a regular file: \" + file);\n            }\n            long size = Files.size(file);\n            if (size == 0 || size > MAX_KEY_FILE_BYTES) {\n                throw new IllegalStateException(\"key file size is out of range: \" + file);\n            }\n            requireOwnerOnlyAccess(file);\n            requireSafeParent(file.getParent());\n            return Files.readAllBytes(file);\n }\n\n    /**\n     * Fails unless the file's POSIX mode grants nothing to group or other and\n     * the file is owned by the current user. On filesystems without POSIX\n     * attributes (where the check cannot be performed) we also fail closed\n     * rather than assuming the file is private.\n     */\n    private static void requireOwnerOnlyAccess(Path file) throws IOException {\n        PosixFileAttributeView view =\n                Files.getFileAttributeView(file, PosixFileAttributeView.class, LinkOption.NOFOLLOW_LINKS);\n        if (view == null) {\n            throw new IllegalStateException(\n                    \"cannot verify permissions of key file (no POSIX attributes): \" + file);\n        }\n        PosixFileAttributes attrs = view.readAttributes();\n        for (PosixFilePermission permission : attrs.permissions()) {\n            if (FORBIDDEN.contains(permission)) {\n                CryptoLog.configProblem(\"key file is accessible to other users: \" + file);\n                throw new IllegalStateException(\n                        \"key file must be readable only by its owner (chmod 600): \" + file);\n            }\n        }\n        UserPrincipal me = file.getFileSystem()\n                .getUserPrincipalLookupService()\n                .lookupPrincipalByName(System.getProperty(\"user.name\", \"\"));\n        if (!attrs.owner().equals(me)) {\n            CryptoLog.configProblem(\"key file is not owned by the current user: \" + file);\n            throw new IllegalStateException(\"key file must be owned by the current user: \" + file);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** A world-writable parent directory lets an attacker replace the key file. */\n    private static void requireSafeParent(Path parent) throws IOException {\n        if (parent == null) {\n            return;\n        }\n        PosixFileAttributeView view = Files.getFileAttributeView(parent, PosixFileAttributeView.class);\n        if (view == null) {\n            return; A caller cannot weaken the work factor below the floor, and an\n *       absurdly high value is also rejected so a configuration value cannot be\n *       turned into a denial-of-service knob.</li>\n *   <li>The salt must be at least {@value #MIN_SALT_BYTES} bytes, must be\n *       unique per passphrase, and must come from\n *       {@link #newSalt()} (the platform CSPRNG). A salt is not secret, but it\n *       is validated for length and bounded.</li>\n *   <li>The passphrase is taken as {@code char[]} and is wiped - along with\n *       every intermediate copy, An attacker with write access to an intermediate directory could swap the file for a symlink between the check and the read operation. While exploiting this requires pre-existing local access, it's still a legitimate security weakness that allows reading arbitrary files into memory as key material—though the cryptographic impact is limited since the attacker controls what gets loaded rather than exfiltrating the key itself.\n\nBut there's a more serious consequence: if an attacker swaps in a file they control, the application derives its encryption key from attacker-chosen bytes, effectively letting them dictate the key. Worse still, if they symlink to a legitimate keystore before the app starts, then swap it back after the app has loaded the key into memory, the GCM tag verification would succeed with the real key, causing the application to decrypt and return plaintext to an attacker who only controls the ciphertext input—a genuine plaintext oracle vulnerability. The attack is complex and requires local write access to the key-file parent directory plus precise timing, but the impact is high: the key is substituted at load time.\n\nNow I'm weighing severity across the findings. JVM property is attacker-influenceable via `-Duser.name=` and does not reflect the real euid. An attacker who can launch the JVM with a spoofed property (or who runs a helper) makes the check compare against an arbitrary username. Real risk: the check is meant to enforce that the key file is owned by the effective user; using the mutable property means a process started with `-Duser.name=root` would pass the check for a root-owned key file, allowing an attacker who can influence JVM args (or a co-located service account that can read the file) to point the app at a file owned by someone else but mode 600 — wait, mode 600 means only the owner can read. If the file is mode 600 and owned by another user, the process can't actually read it → readAllBytes throws IOException → fails anyway. So the practical exploit is limited: the FORBIDDEN permission check already ensures no group/other read, so if the owner isn't us we can't read it anyway. Thus SEC-02 is defense-in-depth / spurious-dependency: Low severity, arguably informational. Still a legitimate finding: the security decision relies on a spoofable JVM property rather than the effective UID; where the file is group-readable it's already caught... Actually the value of the owner check is defense-in-depth on filesystems where POSIX perms are advisory (NFS with all_squash, FUSE mounts) — there the mode bits lie and the owner check is the real control, and it can be bypassed with -Duser.name. That's a fair framing. The security-critical comparison \"is the key file owned by me?\" therefore consults a value the same trust boundary can control: a launcher wrapper, an app server's config, or any library in the same JVM can set user.name to the file owner's name and pass the check for a 0600 file owned by a different user (e.g. a file an attacker placed and chowned to a service account that has since been \"removed\", or in multi-tenant hosts where an attacker controls only the JVM args of a shared app server). Yes — it uses `newInputStream` which follows links by default. And `Files.isRegularFile(file, NOFOLLOW_LINKS)` returns false for a symlink. So at check time the file is a real file, but by read time the path could name a symlink (swapped by an attacker with write access to the parent dir) → readAllBytes would follow it. However, the symlink target's *mode* would be checked... no, the checks already happened before the swap. So the read would follow to an arbitrary path (e.g. /proc/self/... or another user's file) — but reading a 0600 file owned by another user fails with EACCES. The realistic outcome: attacker swaps in a symlink to a file they control (e.g., their own key file) → passes checks earlier because the original path was clean → app loads attacker-chosen key material. Also: attacker with write access to parent could simply delete and replace the file (no symlink needed) — the TOCTOU window is the same; the symlink angle extends reach to files outside the directory. So the core issue = replaceable-key-file window, plus incomplete ancestor check. Also note: an attacker who can write the key directory can already swap the file *before* startup, which no check inside this function can prevent — so the real mitigation is directory permissions (checked) and integrity/attestation. To be fair and calibrated, the concrete gap I can defend is: (a) checks and read are not atomic on the same descriptor, so a swap inside the window defeats all four gates; (b) only the immediate parent is checked for OTHERS_WRITE, so a world-writable grandparent allows replacing the parent directory itself, defeating gate (b). I'll state precisely, with the pre-existing-access caveat so severity is honest (Low).\n\nNow, the ancestor-chain claim: if /var/app (world-writable) contains keys/ (0755, owned by app) containing data.key — an attacker can rename keys/ and create a new keys/ with their own data.key 0600 owned by attacker... wait, owner check requires the file be owned by *our* uid. Attacker creates a file → owned by attacker → owner check fails. Hmm! So the owner check blocks a naive plant unless the attacker is the same uid (in which case they can already write the file). So how do they win?\n\nWinning path: attacker is a *different* uid but has write access to a world-writable ancestor and the target directory is owned by us with 0755 → attacker cannot write inside it, but CAN rename it away (rename requires write on the *parent*, i.e., the world-writable /var/app) and create a symlink keys → /tmp/attacker-dir. Then our path resolves through the attacker's symlink to a directory containing a file owned by... still the attacker's uid → owner check fails. Unless attacker can make a file owned by our uid (only root or setuid can). On filesystems with FUSE/overlay or NFS with all_squash, ownership is spoofable/moot. Or via a bind mount in a user namespace (unprivileged user namespaces + FUSE → attacker can present files with arbitrary uid mapping!). That's a genuine modern path: unprivileged user namespace + FUSE mount lets an attacker present a file with any owner and any mode. In that case *all* the mode/owner gates are attacker-controlled anyway, so the TOCTOU isn't the interesting part.\n\nHonest calibration: the exploitable preconditions are narrow (same-uid process/attacker — for whom the file is already writable — or root/FUSE/namespace tricks that bypass the gates outright). The most concrete residual value of the finding is defense-in-depth: the code's own comment claims swap-protection (\"or be swapped between check and read\") that the implementation does not provide, and the ancestor chain is only partially checked. That's a legitimate Low/Informational finding. I'll report it as Low with an explicit preconditions paragraph so the reader isn't misled. Good.\n\nSEC-02 calibration: same — with -Duser.name spoof, attacker must control JVM launch args (which implies broad control) or run in-process code. And on a POSIX fs with 0600, an unowned file is unreadable anyway. So the check's real purpose is defense-in-depth on non-POSIX-semantics mounts (NFS/FUSE/samba, where \"owner\" is nominal and mode bits are advisory — exactly where an attacker-supplied server controls both owner and mode). On such mounts, the property-based owner check is trivially defeated by any process on the box starting the JVM with `-Duser.name=<expected>`. Keep Low and state the precondition honestly: it weakens the control rather than granting a standalone attack.\n\nSEC-03 is the most concrete of the three: an operator config value (`iterations`) can select 210k, ~2.9× below the current OWASP floor for PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256, and the code's stated justification misattributes the number. Low severity (default is compliant; requires misconfiguration to matter). \n\nOrdering by severity: all Low. I'll order SEC-01 = PBKDF2 floor? Hmm — \"ordered by severity\" and all are Low; within equal severity, order by practical likelihood of impact. I think SEC-01 (weakened key-file gate, TOCTOU) first, SEC-02 (spoofable owner check) second, SEC-03 (KDF floor) third. Actually, likelihood-of-being-the-actual-problem favors SEC-03 (KDF floor is a plain compliance/cryptographic-strength gap that ships in the default API surface), whereas SEC-01/02 are local-precondition defense-in-depth. I'll order: SEC-01 = KDF floor? Let me decide: severity is \"Low\" for all three; I'll order by (a) cryptographic strength impact with no preconditions beyond config = SEC-01, (b) TOCTOU/ancestor gap = SEC-02, (c) spoofable property = SEC-03. Hmm, but is SEC-01 \"Low\" or \"Informational\"? Given default is compliant, some would call it Informational.
